````math
([43](#S5.E43 "In V-D2 Decentralised (natural) gradient descent variational inference with gradient tracking ‣ V-D Decentralised (Natural) Gradient Descent Variational inference for Maximising LM-ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))-([44](#S5.E44 "In V-D2 Decentralised (natural) gradient descent variational inference with gradient tracking ‣ V-D Decentralised (Natural) Gradient Descent Variational inference for Maximising LM-ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
for local estimate $`\{\lambda_{n,k}^{s}\}_{s=1}^{N_{s}}`$. In both
algorithms, every update requires pre-computation of
$`q_{n}^{s,*}(\theta_{n,j}^{s})`$ with the latest $`\lambda_{n}^{s}`$ to
simplify the computation of gradients $`\bm{g}_{i}(\mathcal{L}_{s})`$,
which will be used in
([41](#S5.E41 "In V-D1 Decentralised (natural) gradient descent variational inference with diminishing stepsize ‣ V-D Decentralised (Natural) Gradient Descent Variational inference for Maximising LM-ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))-([44](#S5.E44 "In V-D2 Decentralised (natural) gradient descent variational inference with gradient tracking ‣ V-D Decentralised (Natural) Gradient Descent Variational inference for Maximising LM-ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")).
In the next subsection, we present the derivation of
$`\bm{g}_{i}(\mathcal{L}_{s})`$ at iteration $`i`$ at each sensor $`s`$.

Finally, full implementations of the DeG-VT-DS and DeG-VT-GT are given
in Algorithm
[1](#alg1 "In 0.F.1 Decentralised gradient variational multi-object trackers with with diminishing stepsize (DeG-VT-DS) ‣ Appendix 0.F Details of proposed trackers and pseudocodes ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
and
[2](#alg2 "In 0.F.2 Decentralised gradient variational multi-object trackers with with gradient tracking (DeG-VT-GT) ‣ Appendix 0.F Details of proposed trackers and pseudocodes ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
in Appendix
[0.F](#chapterx1.A6 "Appendix 0.F Details of proposed trackers and pseudocodes ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").

##### VI-A2 Derivation of $`\bm{g}_{i}(\mathcal{L}_{s})`$

For DeG-VT-DS and DeG-VT-GT algorithms,
$`\bm{g}_{i}(\mathcal{L}_{s})\!=\!{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})|_{\lambda_{n}^{s}=\lambda_{n}^{s}(i)}`$,
i.e., the normal gradient with respect to $`\lambda_{n}^{s}`$. Using
([40](#S5.E40 "In V-C1 Properties of local LM-ELBO ℒ_𝑠⁢(𝜆_𝑛) ‣ V-C Decentralisation of LM-ELBO for Multi-sensor Fusion ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
this simplifies to
$`\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})\!=\!\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{F}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s},\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n}^{s}))`$,
where $`\mathcal{F}_{s}`$ is defined in
([35](#S5.E35 "In V-B Justification for use of LM-ELBO over Original ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
and $`\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$ is considered constant and
independent of $`\lambda_{n}^{s}`$ when evaluating the gradient. Its
final form is given below (see Appendix
[0.D](#chapterx1.A4 "Appendix 0.D Derivation of the gradient ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
for detailed derivations):

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{F}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s},\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n}^{s}))\!=\!\frac{-1}{2N_{s}}\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{K}\!\!\!\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\Big(\!\big(\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1}\big)^{\!-1}\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n}\!\Big)\right.`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\left.-\log\big|\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n}\big|+(\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}\!-\!\mu^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})^{\!\top}(\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})^{-1}(\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}\!-\!\mu^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{K}\!\!\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\left[\big(H\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}\!-\!\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}\big)^{\top}\big(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}\big)^{-1}\big(H\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}\!-\!\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}\big)\right.`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\left.\hskip 60.00009pt+\mathrm{Tr}\big(H^{\top}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}H\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n}\big)\right]`$ |  | (46) |

where the following local parameters
$`\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}`$
and
$`\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}`$
are treated as independent of $`\lambda_{n}^{s}`$ during gradient
evaluation at sensor $`s`$

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}=`$ | $`\displaystyle\frac{R_{k}}{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}q_{n}^{s,*}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)},`$ |  | (47) |

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}=`$ | $`\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}Y_{n,j}^{s}q_{n}^{s,*}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)}{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}q_{n}^{s,*}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)}.`$ |  | (48) |

Then, $`{\nabla}_{\!\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$
is evaluated (detailed in Appendix
[0.D](#chapterx1.A4 "Appendix 0.D Derivation of the gradient ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
through its components
$`\nabla_{\!\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$ and
$`\nabla_{\!\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$ with
respect to each local estimate $`\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}`$ and
$`\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n}`$ for $`k\!=\!1,2,...,K`$:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{N_{s}}(\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})^{-1}(\mu^{k*,s}_{n|n-1}-\mu^{k,s}_{n|n})`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad+H^{\top}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}-H\mu^{k,s}_{n|n})`$ |  | (49) |

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N_{s}}\left((\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n})^{-1}-(\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})^{-1}\right)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}H^{\top}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}H`$ |  | (50) |

#### VI-B Decentralised Natural Gradient Variational Trackers

One possible issue with the proposed DeG-VT-DS and DeG-VT-GT in the
previous section is that they use the standard gradient descent; as a
result, the variational parameters are updated by taking small steps in
a Euclidean parameter space, whereas for updating parameters of
distributions, it ignores the information geometry of the posterior
approximation and could lead to slow convergence rate. Natural gradient
scales the traditional gradient with the inverse of its Fisher
Information Matrix (FIM), $`G(\lambda_{n})`$, i.e.,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})=G(\lambda_{n}^{s})^{-1}\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s}).`$ |  | (51) |

where $`\hat{\nabla}`$ denotes the natural gradient, and FIM
$`G(\lambda_{n})`$ is a Riemannian metric for computing distance in the
distribution:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle G(\lambda_{n}^{s})=\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}}\left[\left(\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\ln{q_{n}(X_{n};\lambda_{n}^{s})}\right)\left(\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\ln{q_{n}(X_{n};\lambda_{n}^{s})}\right)^{\top}\right]`$ |  |

For easier computation of the natural gradient, the optimised
distribution parameter is typically defined as the natural parameter of
the exponential family, as we will later define for $`\lambda_{n}^{s}`$
in
([53](#S6.E53 "In VI-B2 Derivation of the natural gradient 𝒈_𝑖⁢(ℒ_𝑠) ‣ VI-B Decentralised Natural Gradient Variational Trackers ‣ VI Decentralised Gradient-based Variational Multi-object Trackers ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")).
The use of the natural gradient is well known to enhance convergence
over standard gradients \[[27](#bib.bib27), [3](#biba.bib3)\].

Hence, we propose a decentralised natural gradient descent scheme where
local sensors collaboratively solve the same optimisation task, but
replacing the standard gradient with the natural gradient in the update
equations in
([41](#S5.E41 "In V-D1 Decentralised (natural) gradient descent variational inference with diminishing stepsize ‣ V-D Decentralised (Natural) Gradient Descent Variational inference for Maximising LM-ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))-([44](#S5.E44 "In V-D2 Decentralised (natural) gradient descent variational inference with gradient tracking ‣ V-D Decentralised (Natural) Gradient Descent Variational inference for Maximising LM-ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
with
$`\bm{g}_{i}(\mathcal{L}_{s})=\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})|_{\lambda_{n}^{s}=\lambda_{n}^{s}(i)}`$.

Subsequently, we propose two decentralised trackers, the Decentralised
Natural Gradient Variational multi-object Trackers with Diminishing
Stepsize (DeNG-VT-DS) and Decentralised Natural Gradient Variational
multi-object Tracker with Gradient Tracking (DeNG-VT-GT), whose full
procedures are given in Algorithm
[3](#alg3 "In 0.F.3 Decentralised natural gradient variational multi-object trackers with with diminishing stepsize (DeNG-VT-DS) ‣ Appendix 0.F Details of proposed trackers and pseudocodes ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
and
[4](#alg4 "In 0.F.4 Decentralised natural gradient variational multi-object trackers with with gradient tracking (DeNG-VT-GT) ‣ Appendix 0.F Details of proposed trackers and pseudocodes ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
in Appendix
[0.F](#chapterx1.A6 "Appendix 0.F Details of proposed trackers and pseudocodes ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").

##### VI-B1 Prediction and update steps

Similar to Section
[VI-A1](#S6.SS1.SSS1 "VI-A1 Prediction and update steps ‣ VI-A Decentralised Gradient Variational Multi-object Trackers ‣ VI Decentralised Gradient-based Variational Multi-object Trackers ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
the predictive prior is
$`\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n,k})=\mathcal{N}(X_{n,k};\mu^{k*,s}_{n|n-1},\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})`$,
where $`\mu^{k*,s}_{n|n-1},\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1}`$ are computed
according to
([10](#S2.E10 "In II-C1 Prediction step in the variational filtering ‣ II-C Variational Filtering for Multi-object Tracking ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")).
In the update step, DeNG-VT-DS follows the update in
([41](#S5.E41 "In V-D1 Decentralised (natural) gradient descent variational inference with diminishing stepsize ‣ V-D Decentralised (Natural) Gradient Descent Variational inference for Maximising LM-ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
while DeNG-VT-GT uses
([43](#S5.E43 "In V-D2 Decentralised (natural) gradient descent variational inference with gradient tracking ‣ V-D Decentralised (Natural) Gradient Descent Variational inference for Maximising LM-ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))-([44](#S5.E44 "In V-D2 Decentralised (natural) gradient descent variational inference with gradient tracking ‣ V-D Decentralised (Natural) Gradient Descent Variational inference for Maximising LM-ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
where each iterative update also requires pre-computing
$`q_{n}^{s,*}(\theta_{n,j}^{s})`$ in parallel using
([22](#S3.E22 "In III-B1 Standard free-form ELBO and centralised CAVI for sensor fusion and tracking ‣ III-B Variational Inference Update for Multi-object Tracking ‣ III Variational Inference with Standard ELBO for Multi-object Tracking ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
to facilitate computing $`\bm{g}_{i}(\mathcal{L}_{s})`$. Here,
$`\bm{g}_{i}(\mathcal{L}_{s})`$ is the natural gradient, with detailed
derivations provided below.

##### VI-B2 Derivation of the natural gradient $`\bm{g}_{i}(\mathcal{L}_{s})`$

First, we can rewrite the predictive prior $`\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n,k})`$ and
the variational distribution $`q_{n}(X_{n,k})`$, $`k=1,...,K`$ at time
step $`n`$ of the $`s`$-th sensor into the form of canonical exponential
family distributions $`\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n,k};\eta_{n,k}^{s})`$ and
$`q_{n}(X_{n,k};\lambda_{n,k}^{s})`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n,k};\eta_{n,k}^{s})=h(X_{n,k})\exp\left({\eta_{n,k}^{s}}^{\top}T(X_{n,k})-A(\eta_{n,k}^{s})\right)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle q_{n}(X_{n,k};\lambda_{n,k}^{s})=h(X_{n,k})\exp\left({\lambda_{n,k}^{s}}^{\top}T(X_{n,k})-A(\lambda_{n,k}^{s})\right)`$ |  |

where $`h(\cdot)`$ is the base function, $`T(\cdot)`$ is the sufficient
statistic, $`A(\cdot)`$ is the log partition function, all for the
Gaussian distribution. The natural parameters $`\eta_{n,k}^{s}`$ and
$`\lambda_{n,k}^{s}`$ are defined as

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\eta_{n,k}^{s}=\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{n,k}^{s,1}\\
\eta_{n,k}^{s,2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}(\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})^{-1}\mu^{k*,s}_{n|n-1}\\
-\frac{1}{2}(\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})^{-1}\end{bmatrix}`$ |  | (52) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\lambda_{n,k}^{s}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{n,k}^{s,1}\\
\lambda_{n,k}^{s,2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}(\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n})^{-1}\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}\\
-\frac{1}{2}(\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n})^{-1}\end{bmatrix}`$ |  | (53) |

Using
([35](#S5.E35 "In V-B Justification for use of LM-ELBO over Original ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
([39](#S5.E39 "In V-C Decentralisation of LM-ELBO for Multi-sensor Fusion ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
([51](#S6.E51 "In VI-B Decentralised Natural Gradient Variational Trackers ‣ VI Decentralised Gradient-based Variational Multi-object Trackers ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
and the property
([40](#S5.E40 "In V-C1 Properties of local LM-ELBO ℒ_𝑠⁢(𝜆_𝑛) ‣ V-C Decentralisation of LM-ELBO for Multi-sensor Fusion ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
the natural gradient of the LM-ELBO simplifies into the following two
parts after canceling the zero terms in
$`\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{F}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s},\rho_{n}^{s})|_{\rho_{n}^{s}=\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n}^{s})}`$:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{1}(\lambda_{n}^{s})+\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$ |  | (54) |

where
$`\mathcal{L}_{s}^{1}(\lambda_{n}^{s})=\frac{1}{N_{s}}\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n};\lambda_{n})}\log\frac{\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n})}{q_{n}(X_{n};\lambda_{n})}`$,
and
$`\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(\lambda_{n}^{s})=\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n};\lambda_{n})q_{n}^{s,*}(\theta_{n}^{s})}\log p(Y_{n}^{s}|\theta_{n}^{s},X_{n})`$,
with $`q_{n}^{s,*}(\theta_{n}^{s})`$ treated as constant that
independent of $`\lambda_{n}^{s}`$ during gradient evaluation.

Here we use two different strategies to compute
$`\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{1}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$
and
$`\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$
in order to avoid calculating the FIM term $`G(\lambda_{n}^{s})^{-1}`$.
Full derivations and the required exponential family properties are
provided in Appendix
[0.E](#chapterx1.A5 "Appendix 0.E Derivation of the natural gradient ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
while only a brief derivation is presented in the remainder of this
section. Specifically, to compute
$`\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{1}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$,
we first compute the standard gradient
$`\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{1}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$, which
has the following simple form:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{1}(\lambda_{n}^{s})=`$ | $`\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{K}\!\!\nabla_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\Big[(\eta_{n,k}^{s}-\lambda_{n,k}^{s})^{\top}\nabla_{\lambda_{n,k}^{s}}A(\lambda_{n,k}^{s})`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+A(\lambda_{n,k}^{s}))-A(\eta_{n,k}^{s}))\Big]`$ |  | (55) |

Then, by using
([51](#S6.E51 "In VI-B Decentralised Natural Gradient Variational Trackers ‣ VI Decentralised Gradient-based Variational Multi-object Trackers ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
and the property that
$`G(\lambda_{n}^{s})=\nabla^{2}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}A(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$,
the natural gradient
$`\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n,k}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{1}(\lambda_{n,k}^{s})`$
for each natural parameter $`\lambda_{n,k}^{s,1}`$ and
$`\lambda_{n,k}^{s,2}`$, $`k=1,...,K`$ is

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n,k}^{s,1}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{1}(\lambda_{n,k}^{s})=\frac{1}{N_{s}}(\eta_{n,k}^{s,1}-\lambda_{n,k}^{s,1})`$ |  | (56) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n,k}^{s,2}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{1}(\lambda_{n,k}^{s})=\frac{1}{N_{s}}(\eta_{n,k}^{s,2}-\lambda_{n,k}^{s,2})`$ |  | (57) |

where the FIM term cancels out without needing computation.

Next, to compute the second component
$`\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$,
we use the method from \[[5](#biba.bib5)\] to avoid a direct computation
of the FIM: define
$`m_{n,k}^{s}=\mathrm{E}_{q(X_{n,k};\lambda_{n,k}^{s})}T(X_{n,k})`$ as
the mean sufficient statistics; then, the natural gradient with respect
to $`m_{n,k}^{s}`$ equals to the gradient with respect to natural
parameters (see Appendix
[0.E](#chapterx1.A5 "Appendix 0.E Derivation of the natural gradient ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
i.e.,
$`\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n}^{s})=\nabla_{m_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(m_{n}^{s})`$.
Therefore, we can compute the standard gradient
$`\nabla_{m_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(m_{n}^{s})`$ instead of
$`\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(\lambda_{n}^{s})`$.
According to \[[5](#biba.bib5)\], each $`m_{n,k}^{s}`$ has the following
relationship with its Gaussian mean and covariance

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle m_{n,k}^{s}=\begin{bmatrix}m_{n,k}^{s,1}\\
m_{n,k}^{s,2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}\\
\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}[\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}]^{\top}+\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n}\end{bmatrix}`$ |  | (58) |

After substituting $`m_{n,k}^{s}`$ from
([58](#S6.E58 "In VI-B2 Derivation of the natural gradient 𝒈_𝑖⁢(ℒ_𝑠) ‣ VI-B Decentralised Natural Gradient Variational Trackers ‣ VI Decentralised Gradient-based Variational Multi-object Trackers ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
and computing the expectations in $`\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(m_{n}^{s})`$
(see Appendix
[0.E](#chapterx1.A5 "Appendix 0.E Derivation of the natural gradient ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{m_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(m_{n}^{s})`$ |  | (59) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{K}\!\!\!\nabla_{m_{n}^{s}}\mathrm{Tr}\Big(\!H^{\top}\!(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}H(m_{n,k}^{s,2}\!-\!m_{n,k}^{s,1}(m_{n,k}^{s,1})^{\top})\!\Big)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{K}\!\!\!\nabla_{m_{n}^{s}}(Hm_{n,k}^{s,1}\!-\!\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{\top}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}(Hm_{n,k}^{s,1}\!-\!\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})`$ |  |  |

Subsequently, the natural gradients with respect to mean parameters
$`m_{n,k}^{s,1}`$ and $`m_{n,k}^{s,2}`$ are

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{m_{n,k}^{s,1}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(m_{n,k}^{s})`$ | $`\displaystyle=H^{\top}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}`$ |  | (60) |

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{m_{n,k}^{s,2}}\mathcal{L}_{s}^{2}(m_{n,k}^{s})`$ | $`\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}H^{\top}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}H`$ |  | (61) |

In sum, the total natural gradients can be obtained by using
([54](#S6.E54 "In VI-B2 Derivation of the natural gradient 𝒈_𝑖⁢(ℒ_𝑠) ‣ VI-B Decentralised Natural Gradient Variational Trackers ‣ VI Decentralised Gradient-based Variational Multi-object Trackers ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
and
([56](#S6.E56 "In VI-B2 Derivation of the natural gradient 𝒈_𝑖⁢(ℒ_𝑠) ‣ VI-B Decentralised Natural Gradient Variational Trackers ‣ VI Decentralised Gradient-based Variational Multi-object Trackers ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))-([61](#S6.E61 "In VI-B2 Derivation of the natural gradient 𝒈_𝑖⁢(ℒ_𝑠) ‣ VI-B Decentralised Natural Gradient Variational Trackers ‣ VI Decentralised Gradient-based Variational Multi-object Trackers ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")):

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n,k}^{s,1}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n,k}^{s})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{s}}\left[(\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})^{-1}\mu^{k*,s}_{n|n-1}-(\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n})^{-1}\mu^{k,s}_{n|n}\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad+H^{\top}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}`$ |  | (62) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n,k}^{s,2}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n,k}^{s})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N_{s}}[(\Sigma^{k,s}_{n|n})^{-1}-(\Sigma^{k*,s}_{n|n-1})^{-1}]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}H^{\top}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}H`$ |  | (63) |

#### VI-C Robust decentralised tracking: explainable performance in limited iterations

Ideally, achieving consensus in the previous time step ensures identical
distributions $`\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n};\eta_{n}^{s})`$ across different
sensors at time step $`n`$. However, when (natural) gradient descent
iterations are limited for efficiency before reaching convergence,
sensors may in practice compute different priors
$`\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n};\eta_{n}^{s})`$. In this case, our decentralised
trackers still perform sensible inference, optimising the same LM-ELBO
in
([37](#S5.E37 "In V-C Decentralisation of LM-ELBO for Multi-sensor Fusion ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
but with a different prior, which can be interpreted as the geometric
average (GA) \[[8](#bib.bib8), [9](#bib.bib9)\] fusion of the individual
sensor priors:
$`\hat{p}_{eff}(X_{n})\propto\prod_{s=1}^{N_{s}}\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n};\eta_{n}^{s})^{1/N_{s}}`$,
as fully derived in Appendix
[0.G](#chapterx1.A7 "Appendix 0.G Handling Non-Convergence: Effective Prior in Decentralised Tracking with Limited Iterations ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").
Thus, it remains a reasonable fused prior. Notably, in our proposed
decentralised gradient-based VTs, this GA fusion occurs automatically
without extra processing steps. This contrasts with traditional GA
fusion approaches which necessitate separate consensus algorithms to
implement a fully distributed GA fusion rule.

### VII Results

This section investigates empirical sensor fusion and tracking
performance of the proposed methods under both fixed and time-varying
sensor networks, with a detailed comparison to the following methods:

##

- C-VT
  Centralised variational multi-object tracker
- DeAA-VT
  Decentralised arithmetic average variational multi-object tracker
- DeC-VT
  Decentralised consensus-based variational multi-object tracker
- DeG-VT-DS
  Decentralised gradient variational multi-object tracker with
  diminishing stepsize
- DeG-VT-GT
  Decentralised gradient variational multi-object tracker with gradient
  tracking
- DeNG-VT-DS
  Decentralised natural gradient variational multi-object tracker with
  diminishing stepsize
- DeNG-VT-GT
  Decentralised natural gradient variational multi-object tracker with
  gradient tracking
- I-VT
  Individual variational multi-object tracker

Specifically, in I-VT, each sensor runs variational multi-object tracker
independently. C-VT is a baseline optimal fusion method that receives
all measurement from all sensors, detailed in Section
[III-B1](#S3.SS2.SSS1 "III-B1 Standard free-form ELBO and centralised CAVI for sensor fusion and tracking ‣ III-B Variational Inference Update for Multi-object Tracking ‣ III Variational Inference with Standard ELBO for Multi-object Tracking ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").
Among them, our proposed methods in this paper are the decentralised
(natural) gradient variational multi-object trackers, including
DeG-VT-DS, DeG-VT-GT, DeNG-VT-DS, and DeNG-VT-GT in Algorithm 1-4 in the
supplementary material. In addition, we compare with DeC-VT algorithm in
\[[22](#bib.bib22)\] to showcase our improvement in communication
efficiency. We also include compare with a commonly-used suboptimal
distributed arithmetic average (AA) fusion strategy \[[9](#bib.bib9),
[10](#bib.bib10)\], where each sensor infers a multi-object posterior
distribution using the variational tracker in \[[17](#bib.bib17)\] based
on local measurements, then a distributed average consensus algorithm is
implemented to fuse the multi-object posteriors from each sensor using
the AA fusion principle.

![Refer to caption](2408.13689v4/figure/sensornetworkscen1ns5meas1.png)

Fig. 1: Sensor networks of dataset 1 and 2 in Scene 1; Red circles are
sensor nodes, grey lines denote their connectivity, and black dots are
an example measurement data of one time step at a single sensor

![Refer to caption](2408.13689v4/figure/est_scene1_all11.png)

Fig. 2: Example tracking performance at one Monte Carlo run of
DeNG-VT-GT (left) and DeAA-VT (right); coloured dotted lines are
estimate, black lines are ground truth and grey ellipses are 95%
confidence interval. The boxes in the right figure mark the track loss
events using DeAA-VT

#### VII-A Performance Metrics

We use the following metrics to evaluate the performance.

##### VII-A1 Generalised optimal sub pattern assignment (GOSPA)

The GOSPA distance \[[39](#bib.bib39)\] is used to evaluate the tracking
accuracy, where the order $`p=1`$, $`\alpha=2`$, and the cut-off
distance $`c=50`$. Concurrently, GOSPA metric returns localisation
errors for well-tracked objects, the missed object errors and false
object errors. Here, we have a fixed number of objects in the scene;
thus, the missed and false object errors denote the track loss rather
than the disappearance or appearance of objects. We define a MGOSPA
metric, which is the mean GOSPA averaged over all sensors and all time
steps.

##### VII-A2 Communication Iteration (CI)

To show the communication cost, we define CI as the total iteration
number that sensors pass messages to its neighbours at a time step,
averaged over total time steps and Monte Carlo runs. Specifically, for
decentralised (natural) gradient-based VB trackers, CI is the total
iteration number of the decentralised (natural) gradient descent
algorithms, which also equals to the variational update iterations at
each time step; For DeC-VT \[[22](#bib.bib22)\], CI equals to the total
variational update iterations at each time step multiplies the consensus
algorithm iterations at each variational update iteration. For the
suboptimal DeAA-VT, CI equals to total iterations of consensus algorithm
performed at one time step.

#### VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity

##### VII-B1 Simulation settings

In Scene 1, we analyse sensor fusion and tracking performance of
compared methods with time-invariant sensor network in two datasets with
different sensor number and detection environments. Two different sensor
networks are simulated as shown in Figure
[1](#S7.F1 "Fig. 1 ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
in which their location and connectivity are randomly generated. All
sensors observe the same surveillance area and follow the NHPP
measurement model in Section
[II-B](#S2.SS2 "II-B NHPP Measurement Model and Association Prior ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
with $`R_{k}^{s}=100\text{I}`$. Specifically, in dataset 1, there are 5
sensors, and for each sensor, the object Poisson rates are set to 2 and
the clutter rate is 500; in dataset 2, we have 30 sensors with object
and clutter Poisson rates being 1 and 1000, which is more challenging
for a single sensor to track objects properly since there is frequent
missed detection and object measurements are buried in clutter.

For all datasets, we consider the case that there are 20 objects in the
surveillance area, moving under the constant velocity dynamical model
defined in Section
[II-A](#S2.SS1 "II-A Dynamical Model ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
with parameters being $`F_{n,k}^{d}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\tau\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},Q_{n,k}^{d}=36\begin{bmatrix}\tau^{3}/3&\tau^{2}/2\\
\tau^{2}/2&\tau\end{bmatrix}`$ ($`d=1,2`$). The total time steps are 50,
and the time interval between observations is $`\tau=1`$s. To verify the
robustness of the compared algorithms, we simulate 50 Monte Carlo (MC)
runs for each dataset. In particular, for dataset 1, each MC run
generate different ground-truth tracks and measurements according to the
defined parameter settings, while in dataset 2, we have 50 different
measurement data generated with the same ground-truth tracks shown in
Figure
[1](#S7.F1 "Fig. 1 ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").

Other general parameter settings are as follows. For DeNG-VT-GT, the
fixed stepsize $`\alpha=0.8`$ for both dataset 1 and 2. For DeG-VT-GT,
$`\alpha`$ is set to 5 and 10 for dataset 1 and 2, respectively. In the
case of DeG-VT-DS, we apply a diminishing stepsize
$`\alpha_{i}=1/(i+1)^{\kappa}`$, where $`i`$ denotes the iteration
number. As studied in \[[37](#bib.bib37)\], the condition
$`\kappa\in(0,1]`$ ensures convergence to a stationary point, and here
we set $`\kappa`$ to 0.1 and 0.01 for dataset 1 and 2, respectively. We
present results of DeNG-VT-DS with two different diminishing stepsizes,
denoted as DeNG-VT-DS1 and DeNG-VT-DS2. For DeNG-VT-DS1, we adopt
$`\alpha_{i}=1/(i+1)^{\kappa}`$ with $`\kappa=0.5`$ and 0.1 for dataset
1 and 2, respectively. For DeNG-VT-DS2, we apply a fine-tuned
diminishing stepsize $`\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon/(i+1)^{\kappa}`$ that may
converge faster, with $`\varepsilon=20`$, $`\kappa=2`$ for both dataset
1 and 2.

![Refer to caption](2408.13689v4/figure/conver_gospa_2all3.png)

Fig. 3: GOSPA over iteration number at a single time step; lines and
shaded area are mean and $`\pm 1`$ standard deviation of GOSPA value
averaged over all sensors, respectively.

##### VII-B2 Result 1: analysis of convergence speed of decentralised gradient-based variational trackers at a single time step

In the first simulation, we select a single time step measurement data
from one MC run in both dataset 1 and 2 to perform inference tasks to
analyse the convergence performance of the proposed decentralised
gradient-based methods, including DeNG-VT-GT, DeNG-VT-DS, DeG-VT-GT,
DeG-VT-DS. To make a fair comparison, we assume the same converged
variational distribution at the previous time step for all methods such
that they have the same predictive prior. All other settings are the
same as in Section
[VII-B1](#S7.SS2.SSS1 "VII-B1 Simulation settings ‣ VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").

The convergence speed of the proposed methods is evaluated using GOSPA
values, with the mean and standard deviation plotted across all local
sensors over iterations, as shown in Figure
[3](#S7.F3 "Fig. 3 ‣ VII-B1 Simulation settings ‣ VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").
The standard deviations of all compared methods gradually converge to
zero, indicating that they reach consensus and each sensor shares the
same estimates. Across all datasets, DeNG-VT-GT demonstrates the fastest
convergence, followed by DeNG-VT-DS2, DeNG-VT-DS1, DeG-VT-GT, and
DeG-VT-DS, with DeG-VT-DS showing significantly slower convergence than
the others. While DeNG-VT-DS2 accelerates convergence due to its
fine-turned diminishing step size compared to DeNG-VT-DS1, it deviates
very slightly from the centralised C-VT solution. Meanwhile, all other
methods match the performance of C-VT, empirically demonstrating their
equivalence in tracking performance to C-VT.

![Refer to caption](2408.13689v4/figure/mean_gospaALL4.png)

Fig. 4: GOSPA over 50 time steps; for all methods, lines are means of
GOSPA averaged over all sensors and shaded areas indicate $`\pm 1`$
standard deviation. Y-axis is log-scale.

##### VII-B3 Result 2: comparison of all methods for one single MC run

Having assessed the performance of the proposed methods at a single time
step, we now extend this analysis over all time steps in a single MC run
to evaluate convergence and and communication efficiency of DeNG-VT-GT,
DeNG-VT-DS, DeG-VT-GT, and compare their tracking accuracy with other
methods. We exclude DeG-VT-DS from this evaluation due to its much
slower convergence speed, as detailed in Result 1 in Section
[VII-B2](#S7.SS2.SSS2 "VII-B2 Result 1: analysis of convergence speed of decentralised gradient-based variational trackers at a single time step ‣ VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").

Figure
[2](#S7.F2 "Fig. 2 ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
illustrates mean GOSPA with its one standard deviation over 50 time
steps for each compared methods for one MC run in both dataset 1 and 2.
The subscript of each method in the figure legend represents the
iteration number, i.e., the CI metric, to reflect their communication
cost. The results show that all methods except I-VT achieve zero
standard deviation at each time step, indicating that all sensor nodes
consistently converge to the same values, thus demonstrating their
capability to reach a local optimum. Most importantly, Figure
[4](#S7.F4 "Fig. 4 ‣ VII-B2 Result 1: analysis of convergence speed of decentralised gradient-based variational trackers at a single time step ‣ VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
confirms empirically the equivalence in tracking performance at every
time step between the centralised fusion C-VT and our proposed
decentralised solutions, including DeNG-VT-GT, DeNG-VT-DS, and
DeG-VT-GT. The significant discrepancy in mean GOSPA between the
suboptimal DeAA-VT and our gradient-based methods highlights our
superior tracking accuracy. Notably, DeNG-VT-GT not only achieves lower
GOSPA values with the same communication cost as DeAA-VT but also
matches the performance of C-VT with much lower communication cost
compared to other decentralised gradient-based methods.

To show the difference in tracking accuracy more directly, Figure
[2](#S7.F2 "Fig. 2 ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
plots the estimates of DeNG-VT-GT and DeAA-VT. The results demonstrate
that DeNG-VT-GT consistently tracks all targets with high accuracy,
whereas DeAA-VT frequently loses track and exhibits greater uncertainty
in its estimates.

##### VII-B4 Result 3: Tracking and fusion performance over all 50 runs

We verify the robustness of the proposed and compared methods by testing
it over 50 Monte Carlo runs in two different datasets under the general
settings in Section
[VII-B1](#S7.SS2.SSS1 "VII-B1 Simulation settings ‣ VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").
Table
[I](#S7.T1 "TABLE I ‣ VII-B3 Result 2: comparison of all methods for one single MC run ‣ VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
and
[II](#S7.T2 "TABLE II ‣ VII-B3 Result 2: comparison of all methods for one single MC run ‣ VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
show the performance of the compared methods in both tracking accuracy
and communication efficiency. We record the mean and one standard
deviation of MGOSPA and its submetric (location error, missed object and
false object error), averaged over 50 runs. For both datasets, we can
see that C-VT and all versions of proposed (natural) gradient based
methods show very accurate tracking. In contrast, the tracking accuracy
of I-VT and DeAA-VTs is much lower. The estimation results also confirm
the equivalence in tracking performance of the proposed DeNG-VT-GT,
DeNG-VT-DS, DeG-VT-GT, DeG-VT-DS with the centralised C-VT solution when
it converges.

With regards to communication costs, we can see from CI values the great
advantage of the proposed DeNG-VT-GT compared with the DeC-VT,
DeNG-VT-DS, DeG-VT-GT, and DeG-VT-DS, under the same optimal tracking
accuracy. Compared to the suboptimal DeAA-VT method, we can see that our
method still greatly outperforms DeAA-VT in tracking accuracy even using
the same communication iteration number, which showcases its advantages
in both tracking accuracy and communication efficiency.

#### VII-C Scene 2: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under time-varying network connectivity

In Scene 2, we simulate a more challenging scenario of a time-varying
heterogeneous sensor network in which their location and connectivity
are changing over time as shown in Figure
[5](#S7.F5 "Fig. 5 ‣ VII-C Scene 2: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under time-varying network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").
In the surveillance area, there are 50 targets moving under the constant
velocity model in Section
[II-A](#S2.SS1 "II-A Dynamical Model ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
with parameters being $`F_{n,k}^{d}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\tau\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},Q_{n,k}^{d}=25\begin{bmatrix}\tau^{3}/3&\tau^{2}/2\\
\tau^{2}/2&\tau\end{bmatrix}`$ ($`d=1,2`$). All sensors observe the same
surveillance area and follow the NHPP measurement model in Section
[II-B](#S2.SS2 "II-B NHPP Measurement Model and Association Prior ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
with $`R_{k}^{s}=100\text{I}`$. Specifically, we consider 10
heterogeneous sensors of different detection ability, with their clutter
rate ranging from 100 to 1000 while the target rate for all sensors are
one, meaning that some sensors’ measurements are heavily cluttered. To
verify the robustness of the compared algorithms, we simulate 50 MC runs
with different ground-truth tracks and measurements according to the
parameter settings. For all datasets, the total time steps are 50, and
the time interval between observations is $`\tau=1`$s.

For DeNG-VT-GT, the fixed stepsize $`\alpha=0.8`$. For DeG-VT-GT,
$`\alpha=10`$. In DeG-VT-DS, we apply a diminishing step size
$`1/(i+1)^{\kappa}`$, where $`\kappa=0.5`$, and $`i`$ denotes the
iteration number. For DeNG-VT-DS, we implement both the diminishing
stepsize $`1/(i+1)^{\kappa}`$ with $`\kappa=0.5`$, and a self-tuned
diminishing stepsize $`\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon/(i+1)^{\kappa}`$ with
$`\varepsilon=20`$, $`\kappa=1`$. The latter provides potentially faster
convergence.

![Refer to caption](2408.13689v4/figure/sensornetworkall2.png)

Fig. 5: Time varying sensor networks; Red circles are sensor nodes and
grey lines indicate their connectivity. Black dots are measurements
received at 15th time step from 1st sensor (left) and 38th time step
from 10th sensor (right)

##### VII-C1 Result 1: analysis of convergence speed of decentralised gradient-based variational trackers at a single time step

First, we select a single time step measurement data from one MC run to
analyse the convergence performance of the proposed decentralised
gradient-based methods. Figure
[6](#S7.F6 "Fig. 6 ‣ VII-C3 Result 3: Tracking and fusion performance over all 50 runs ‣ VII-C Scene 2: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under time-varying network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
shows that the standard deviations of all compared methods gradually
converge to zero, indicating that they reach consensus and each sensor
shares the same estimates. Across all datasets, DeNG-VT-GT converges the
fastest, followed by DeNG-VT-DS2, DeNG-VT-DS1, DeG-VT-GT, and DeG-VT-DS.
Meanwhile, all methods match the performance of C-VT, empirically
demonstrating their equivalence in tracking performance to the
centralised C-VT solution.

##### VII-C2 Result 2: comparison of all methods for one single MC run

Having assessed the performance of the proposed methods at a single time
step, we now extend this analysis over all time steps in a single MC run
to evaluate convergence and and communication efficiency of DeNG-VT-GT,
DeNG-VT-DS, DeG-VT-GT, and compare their tracking accuracy with other
methods. Since DeG-VT-DS showing significantly slower convergence than
the others in Section
[VII-C1](#S7.SS3.SSS1 "VII-C1 Result 1: analysis of convergence speed of decentralised gradient-based variational trackers at a single time step ‣ VII-C Scene 2: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under time-varying network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
we exclude DeG-VT-DS from this evaluation.

Figure
[2](#S7.F2 "Fig. 2 ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
illustrates mean GOSPA with its one standard deviation over 50 time
steps for each compared methods. The subscript of each method in the
figure legend represents the iteration number. The results show that all
methods except I-VT achieve zero standard deviation at each time step,
indicating that all sensor nodes reach consensus and consistently
converge to the same values. As shown in fixed network scenarios in
Section
[VII-B](#S7.SS2 "VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
it shows in Figure
[4](#S7.F4 "Fig. 4 ‣ VII-B2 Result 1: analysis of convergence speed of decentralised gradient-based variational trackers at a single time step ‣ VII-B Scene 1: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under fixed network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
that our proposed decentralised solutions are empirically equivalence in
tracking performance to the C-VT. Additional, DeNG-VT-GT again shows
much better tracking accuracy under the comparable communication cost as
DeAA-VT, and are much efficient with regards to communication cost
compared to other decentralised gradient-based methods.

##### VII-C3 Result 3: Tracking and fusion performance over all 50 runs

We verify the robustness of the proposed method by testing it over 50
Monte Carlo runs with different measurement sets. Table
[III](#S7.T3 "TABLE III ‣ VII-C3 Result 3: Tracking and fusion performance over all 50 runs ‣ VII-C Scene 2: Distributed Sensor fusion and multi-object tracking under time-varying network connectivity ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
shows the performance of the compared methods in both tracking accuracy
and communication efficiency. We can see that C-VT, DeC-VT, and all
versions of DeNG-VTs show very accurate tracking, The centralized method
C-VT and several decentralised variants, including DeC-VT, the
DeNG-VT-GT, DeNG-VT-DS2, and DeG-VT-GT, all obtain the same performance
metrics with the same tracking accuracy and no missed or false targets.
DeNG-VT-DS1 shows similar performance to the optimal group but with a
marginally higher MGOSPA, indicating a slight decrease in efficiency. In
contrast, DeAA-VT and I-VT exhibit significantly poorer performance with
much higher MGOSPA values and substantial numbers of missed and false
detections. The estimation results also confirm the equivalence of the
proposed DeNG-VT with the centralised C-VT solution when it converges.

It is observed that, DeNG-VT-GT, can achieve performance on par with the
centralised C-VT, requiring less communication cost compared to other
methods, thus highlighting their potential for efficient and accurate
tracking in scenarios requiring minimal communication overhead.

TABLE III: Performance of compared methods in Scene 2

### VIII Conclusion

This paper presents decentralised multi-object tracking algorithms for
cluttered environments in time-varying sensor networks. Our approaches
achieve tracking performance on par with centralised fusion, outperform
suboptimal distributed fusion strategies in accuracy, and greatly reduce
communication costs compared to existing average consensus VT methods.
Furthermore, our decentralised trackers remain robust under practical
constraints, such as limited gradient descent iterations, while still
delivering reliable and explainable inference. Future improvements
include the integration of new advanced decentralised optimisation
techniques, and extending this framework to accommodate unknown numbers
of objects and multimodal sensors with varying spatial coverage.

Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks

for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks: Additional Notes

## Appendix 0.A Derivations of centralised CAVI

In this part, we present detailed derivation for the update step of
$`q_{n}(X_{n})`$ and $`q_{n}(\theta_{n})`$ in the centralised coordinate
ascent variational inference (CAVI) in Section
[III-B1](#S3.SS2.SSS1 "III-B1 Standard free-form ELBO and centralised CAVI for sensor fusion and tracking ‣ III-B Variational Inference Update for Multi-object Tracking ‣ III Variational Inference with Standard ELBO for Multi-object Tracking ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").

#### 0.A.1 Update for $`q_{n}(X_{n})`$

First we present the update for $`q_{n}(X_{n})`$. According to the
standard CAVI update rule in \[[30](#bib.bib30)\], we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
q_{n}(X_{n})\propto\text{exp}\left(\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(\theta_{n})}\log\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n},\theta_{n},Y_{n})\right)
``` |  | (64) |

where the expression of $`\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n},\theta_{n},Y_{n})`$ is
written as:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n},\theta_{n},Y_{n})`$ | $`\displaystyle=p(Y_{n}|\theta_{n},X_{n})p(\theta_{n}|M_{n})\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n}).`$ |  | (65) |

Thus, we can further derive the update as follows using
([65](#chapterx1.A1.E65 "In 0.A.1 Update for q_n⁢(X_n) ‣ Appendix 0.A Derivations of centralised CAVI ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
([3](#S2.E3 "In II-B NHPP Measurement Model and Association Prior ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
([4](#S2.E4 "In II-B NHPP Measurement Model and Association Prior ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
and the expression of $`\ell({Y}_{n,j}^{s}|X_{n,k})`$ in
([5](#S2.E5 "In II-B NHPP Measurement Model and Association Prior ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")):

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle q_{n}(X_{n})`$ | $`\displaystyle\propto\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n})\text{exp}\left(\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(\theta_{n})}\log p(Y_{n}|\theta_{n},X_{n})\right)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n})\text{exp}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{s}}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s})}\log\ell({Y}_{n,j}^{s}|X_{n,\theta_{n,j}^{s}})`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n})\text{exp}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{s}}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)\log\ell({Y}_{n,j}^{s}|X_{n,k})`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\propto\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n})\text{exp}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{s}}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}\biggl[\sum_{k=1}^{K}q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)\biggl[-\frac{1}{2}(Y_{n,j}^{s}-HX_{n,k})^{\top}(R_{k}^{s})^{-1}(Y_{n,j}^{s}-HX_{n,k})\biggr]+q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=0)\log\frac{1}{V^{s}}\biggl]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\propto\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n})\text{exp}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{s}}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}-\frac{1}{2}(Y_{n,j}^{s}-HX_{n,k})^{\top}\left(\frac{R_{k}^{s}}{q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)}\right)^{-1}(Y_{n,j}^{s}-HX_{n,k})`$ |  | (66) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\propto\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n})\text{exp}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{s}}-\frac{1}{2}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}-HX_{n,k})^{\top}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}}^{-1}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}-HX_{n,k})`$ |  | (67) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\propto\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n})\text{exp}\sum_{k=1}^{K}-\frac{1}{2}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k}-HX_{n,k})^{\top}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k}}^{-1}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k}-HX_{n,k})`$ |  | (68) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\propto\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n})\prod_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{N}\left(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k};HX_{n,k},\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu^{k}_{n}\right),`$ |  | (69) |

where the results from lines
([66](#chapterx1.A1.E66 "In 0.A.1 Update for q_n⁢(X_n) ‣ Appendix 0.A Derivations of centralised CAVI ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
to
([67](#chapterx1.A1.E67 "In 0.A.1 Update for q_n⁢(X_n) ‣ Appendix 0.A Derivations of centralised CAVI ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
and from lines
([67](#chapterx1.A1.E67 "In 0.A.1 Update for q_n⁢(X_n) ‣ Appendix 0.A Derivations of centralised CAVI ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
to
([68](#chapterx1.A1.E68 "In 0.A.1 Update for q_n⁢(X_n) ‣ Appendix 0.A Derivations of centralised CAVI ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
are computed according to the rule of calculating the summation of
quadratic forms (the Lemma E.1 in Appendix E in \[[1](#biba.bib1)\]),
that is, for symmetric and invertible matrix
$`C_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}`$, and vectors
$`x,m_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times 1}`$ ($`i=1,2,...,N`$), we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^{N}-\frac{1}{2}(x-m_{i})^{\top}&C_{i}^{-1}(x-m_{i})=-\frac{1}{2}(x-\mu)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(x-\mu)+\frac{1}{2}\mu^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\mu-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}^{\top}C_{i}^{-1}m_{i},\\
\Sigma&=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}^{-1}\right)^{-1},\ \ \ \ \ \ \mu=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}^{-1}m_{i}.\end{aligned}`$ |  | (70) |

The pseudo-measurements and covariances in
([67](#chapterx1.A1.E67 "In 0.A.1 Update for q_n⁢(X_n) ‣ Appendix 0.A Derivations of centralised CAVI ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))-([69](#chapterx1.A1.E69 "In 0.A.1 Update for q_n⁢(X_n) ‣ Appendix 0.A Derivations of centralised CAVI ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
are computed using the above quadratic summation result as follows:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}=\frac{R_{k}^{s}}{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)},\quad\quad\quad\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}Y_{n,j}^{s}q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)}{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)}.`$ |  | (71) |

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k}=`$ | $`\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{s}}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}\right)^{-1}=\left(\sum_{s=1}^{N_{s}}\left((R_{k}^{s})^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)\right)\right)^{-1},`$ |  | (72) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k}=`$ | $`\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{s}}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{s}}(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s})^{-1}\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muY\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k,s}=\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muR\mkern-3.5mu}\mkern 3.5mu_{n}^{k}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{s}}\Big((R_{k}^{s})^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k)Y_{n,j}^{s}\Big).`$ |  | (73) |

#### 0.A.2 Update for $`q_{n}(\theta_{n})`$

Next, we present the derivation for $`q_{n}(\theta_{n})`$. According to
the standard CAVI update rule in \[[30](#bib.bib30)\] and expression of
$`\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n},\theta_{n},Y_{n})`$ in
([65](#chapterx1.A1.E65 "In 0.A.1 Update for q_n⁢(X_n) ‣ Appendix 0.A Derivations of centralised CAVI ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
we have:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle q_{n}(\theta_{n})\propto`$ | $`\displaystyle\text{exp}\left(\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n})}\log\hat{p}_{n}(X_{n},\theta_{n},Y_{n})\right)`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\propto`$ | $`\displaystyle\text{exp}\left(\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n})}\log p(\theta_{n}|M_{n})p(Y_{n}|\theta_{n},X_{n})\right)`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{N_{s}}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{n}^{s}}\text{exp}\left(\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n})}\log p(\theta_{n,j}^{s})p(Y_{n,j}^{s}|X_{n,\theta_{n,j}^{s}})\right)`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\propto`$ | $`\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{N_{s}}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{n}}q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s})`$ |  | (74) |

In the following, we present detailed derivations for
$`q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s})`$, using expressions of
$`p(\theta_{n,j}^{s})`$ in
([6](#S2.E6 "In II-B NHPP Measurement Model and Association Prior ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
and $`\ell({Y}_{n,j}^{s}|X_{n,k})`$ in
([5](#S2.E5 "In II-B NHPP Measurement Model and Association Prior ‣ II Problem Formulation and Modelling ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")):

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle q_{n}(\theta_{n,j}^{s})=\exp\left(\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n})}\log p(\theta_{n,j}^{s})p(Y_{n,j}^{s}|X_{n,\theta_{n,j}^{s}})\right)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n})}\log\sum_{k=0}^{K}\Lambda_{k}^{s}\ell^{s}(Y_{n,j}^{s}|X_{n,k})\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k]\right)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\exp\left(\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n})}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\log\Lambda_{k}^{s}\ell^{s}(Y_{n,j}^{s}|X_{n,k})\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k]\right)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{s}}{V^{s}}\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=0]+\exp\left(\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n})}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\log\Lambda_{k}^{s}\mathcal{N}(Y_{n,j}^{s};HX_{n,k},R_{k}^{s})\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k]\right)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{s}}{V^{s}}\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=0]+\exp\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bigg[\log\Lambda_{k}^{s}+\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n})}\log\mathcal{N}(Y_{n,j}^{s};HX_{n,k},R_{k}^{s})\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k]\bigg]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{s}}{V^{s}}\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=0]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad+\exp\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bigg[\log\Lambda_{k}^{s}+\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{E}_{q_{n}(X_{n,k})}(Y_{n,j}^{s}-HX_{n,k})^{\top}(R_{k}^{s})^{-1}(Y_{n,j}^{s}-HX_{n,k})+\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{D}{\det R_{k}^{s}}}}\right)\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k]\bigg]\ `$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{s}}{V^{s}}\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=0]+\exp\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bigg[\log\Lambda_{k}^{s}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\left((Y_{n,j}^{s}-H\mu_{n|n}^{k})^{\top}(R_{k}^{s})^{-1}(Y_{n,j}^{s}-H\mu_{n|n}^{k})+\Tr((R_{k}^{s})^{-1}H\Sigma_{n|n}^{k}H^{\top})\right)+\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{D}{\det R_{k}^{s}}}}\bigg)\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k]\bigg]\ `$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{s}}{V^{s}}\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=0]+\exp\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\log\Lambda_{k}^{s}+\bigg(\log\mathcal{N}(Y_{n,j}^{s};H\mu_{n|n}^{k},R_{k}^{s})-0.5\Tr((R_{k}^{s})^{-1}H\Sigma_{n|n}^{k}H^{\top})\bigg)\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k]\right]\ `$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{s}}{V^{s}}\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=0]+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\Lambda_{k}^{s}l_{k}^{s}\delta[\theta_{n,j}^{s}=k]`$ |  | (75) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle l_{k}^{s}=\mathcal{N}(Y_{n,j}^{s};H\mu_{n|n}^{k},R_{k}^{s})\text{exp}(-0.5\text{Tr}({(R_{k}^{s})}^{-1}H\Sigma_{n|n}^{k}H^{\top}))`$ |  | (76) |

where the second line to third line follows from the fact that only one
of $`\delta[\theta_{n,j}=k]`$ for $`k=0,1,...,K`$ equals $`1`$, with the
rest being zero.

## Appendix 0.B Supplementary Properties and Proofs of LM-ELBO

#### 0.B.1 Alternative proof of Property 2

Here we give a proof of Property 2 using the ELBO definition in
([15](#S3.E15 "In III-A Variational Inference for General Problem Settings ‣ III Variational Inference with Standard ELBO for Multi-object Tracking ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
and $`q(\theta;\rho^{*}(\lambda))=q^{*}(\theta)`$. First, recall from
Section
[IV-A](#S4.SS1 "IV-A Definition of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")
that the parametric form $`q(\theta;\rho)`$, as adopted in the ELBO in
([15](#S3.E15 "In III-A Variational Inference for General Problem Settings ‣ III Variational Inference with Standard ELBO for Multi-object Tracking ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
encompasses the optimal distribution $`q^{*}(\theta)`$ in
([25](#S4.E25 "In IV-A Definition of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
i.e.,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle q^{*}(\theta)\propto`$ | $`\displaystyle\exp\left(\mathrm{E}_{q(X;\lambda)}\log f(X,\theta,Y)\right).`$ |  |

Additionally, in Section
[IV-A](#S4.SS1 "IV-A Definition of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
$`\rho^{*}(\lambda)`$ is dented as one parameter value that recovers the
$`q^{*}(\theta)`$ such that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle q(\theta;\rho^{*})=q^{*}(\theta)=\frac{\exp\left(\mathrm{E}_{q(X;\lambda)}\log f(X,\theta,Y)\right)}{Z(\lambda)}`$ |  | (77) |

where $`Z(\lambda)`$ is the normalisation constant that does not depend
on $`\theta`$ or $`X`$.

Using
([15](#S3.E15 "In III-A Variational Inference for General Problem Settings ‣ III Variational Inference with Standard ELBO for Multi-object Tracking ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\rho}\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)=`$ | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\rho}\mathrm{E}_{q(X;\lambda)q(\theta;\rho)}\log f(X,\theta,Y)-\nabla_{\rho}\mathrm{E}_{q(\theta;\rho)}\log q(\theta;\rho),`$ |  | (78) |

as $`\nabla_{\rho}\mathrm{E}_{q(X;\lambda)}\log q(X;\lambda)=0`$. The
second term in
([78](#chapterx1.A2.E78 "In 0.B.1 Alternative proof of Property 2 ‣ Appendix 0.B Supplementary Properties and Proofs of LM-ELBO ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
can be further simplified as

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\rho}\mathrm{E}_{q(\theta;\rho)}\log q(\theta;\rho)=`$ | $`\displaystyle\int\nabla_{\rho}\left(q(\theta;\rho)\log q(\theta;\rho)\right)d\theta`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\int\left(\nabla_{\rho}q(\theta;\rho)\log q(\theta;\rho)+\nabla_{\rho}q(\theta;\rho)\right)d\theta`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\int\nabla_{\rho}q(\theta;\rho)\log q(\theta;\rho)d\theta+\cancelto{0}{\nabla_{\rho}1}.`$ |  | (79) |

The first term in
([78](#chapterx1.A2.E78 "In 0.B.1 Alternative proof of Property 2 ‣ Appendix 0.B Supplementary Properties and Proofs of LM-ELBO ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
is

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\rho}\mathrm{E}_{q(X;\lambda)q(\theta;\rho)}\log f(X,\theta,Y)=`$ | $`\displaystyle\int\nabla_{\rho}q(\theta;\rho)\mathrm{E}_{q(X;\lambda)}\log f(X,\theta,Y)d\theta`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\int\nabla_{\rho}q(\theta;\rho)(\log q(\theta;\rho^{*})+\log Z(\lambda))d\theta`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle=`$ | $`\displaystyle\int\nabla_{\rho}q(\theta;\rho)\log q(\theta;\rho^{*})d\theta+\cancelto{0}{\nabla_{\rho}\log Z(\lambda)},`$ |  | (80) |

where the second last line is obtained using
([77](#chapterx1.A2.E77 "In 0.B.1 Alternative proof of Property 2 ‣ Appendix 0.B Supplementary Properties and Proofs of LM-ELBO ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")).
Subtracting
([79](#chapterx1.A2.E79 "In 0.B.1 Alternative proof of Property 2 ‣ Appendix 0.B Supplementary Properties and Proofs of LM-ELBO ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
from
([80](#chapterx1.A2.E80 "In 0.B.1 Alternative proof of Property 2 ‣ Appendix 0.B Supplementary Properties and Proofs of LM-ELBO ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
yields the gradient in
([78](#chapterx1.A2.E78 "In 0.B.1 Alternative proof of Property 2 ‣ Appendix 0.B Supplementary Properties and Proofs of LM-ELBO ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")):

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\rho}\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)=\int\nabla_{\rho}q(\theta;\rho)(\log q(\theta;\rho^{*})-\log q(\theta;\rho))d\theta.`$ |  |

Finally, we conclude the proof as follows

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\rho}\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)|_{\rho=\rho^{*}}=\int\nabla_{\rho}q(\theta;\rho^{*})|_{\rho=\rho^{*}}\times 0\ d\theta=0.`$ |  | (81) |

#### 0.B.2 Proof and analysis of Property 5

Here we verify this property on a case-by-case basis. The global maximum
case is straightforward: If $`\lambda^{*}`$ is a global maximum of
$`\mathcal{L}(\lambda)`$, then
$`\mathcal{L}(\lambda=\lambda^{*})=\max_{\lambda}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)`$.
Substituting $`\mathcal{L}`$ on the left and right hand sides with
([26](#S4.E26 "In IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
and
([27](#S4.E27 "In IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
respectively, yields
$`\mathcal{F}(\lambda=\lambda^{*},\rho=\rho^{*}(\lambda^{*}))=\max_{\lambda}\max_{\rho}\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)`$,
confirming the global maximum of $`\mathcal{F}`$. The local maximum case
also follows from
([26](#S4.E26 "In IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
and
([27](#S4.E27 "In IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")).
Intuitively, if $`\mathcal{L}(\eta=\eta^{*})`$ is maximal in a small
neighbourhood of $`\lambda^{*}`$, then
$`\mathcal{F}(\lambda=\lambda^{*},\rho=\rho^{*}(\eta^{*}))`$ achieves
the maximum of $`\mathcal{F}`$ for the corresponding vicinity of
$`\lambda^{*}`$ across all $`\rho`$, and consequently, in a small
neighbourhood of $`[\lambda^{*},\rho^{*}(\lambda^{*})]`$, validating the
local maximum. Finally, the stationary point case is confirmed by noting
that $`\nabla_{\lambda}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)|_{\lambda=\lambda^{*}}=0`$
leads to
$`\nabla_{\lambda}\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)|^{\lambda=\lambda^{*}}_{\rho=\rho^{*}(\lambda^{*})}=0`$,
as per
([29](#S4.E29 "In IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")).
Further,
([28](#S4.E28 "In IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
ensures that
$`\nabla_{\rho}\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)|_{\rho=\rho^{*}(\lambda)}=0`$
for all $`\lambda`$, including $`\lambda^{*}`$. Therefore, both
$`\nabla_{\lambda}\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)`$ and
$`\nabla_{\rho}\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)`$ are zero at
$`[\lambda^{*},\rho^{*}(\lambda^{*})]`$, verifying $`\mathcal{F}`$’s
stationary point.

This optimality alignment property demonstrates that any optimum found
by optimising $`\mathcal{L}(\lambda)`$ is inherently an optimum within
the conventional ELBO $`\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)`$, thereby validating
the optimisation of our LM-ELBO. We further note that this optimality
property directly suggests that a distinctive optimal point
$`\lambda^{*}`$ of $`\mathcal{L}(\lambda)`$ results in a distinctive
optimal point $`[\lambda^{*},\rho^{*}(\lambda^{*})]`$ of
$`\mathcal{F}`$, ensuring our LM-ELBO does not introduce extra
suboptimal points—like local maxima or stationary points—where
optimisation algorithms could potentially stagnate, and may even
mitigate such risks.

#### 0.B.3 An additional property of LM-ELBO

One previously established property in \[[2](#biba.bib2)\] for the KLC
bound suggests:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)\leq\mathcal{L}(\lambda)\leq\log\int f(X,\theta,Y)dXd\theta,`$ |  | (82) |

indicating that the LM-ELBO provides a tighter bound on the log evidence
than the conventional ELBO (assuming $`f`$ is the joint density).

Here we present a simple proof using the Property 1 in Section
[IV-B](#S4.SS2 "IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks").
First, the left inequality follows directly from
([27](#S4.E27 "In IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")).
To prove the right inequality, we apply Jensen’s inequality to the ELBO
definition in
([15](#S3.E15 "In III-A Variational Inference for General Problem Settings ‣ III Variational Inference with Standard ELBO for Multi-object Tracking ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
yielding:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\log\mathrm{E}_{q(X;\lambda)q(\theta;\rho)}\left[\frac{f(X,\theta,Y)}{q(X;\lambda)q(\theta;\rho)}\right]=\log\int f(X,\theta,Y)dXd\theta,`$ |  | (83) |

where the right hand side equals $`\log p(Y)`$ if $`f`$ represents
$`p(X,\theta,Y)`$, highlighting that the ELBO is a lower bound on the
log marginal likelihood (regardless of the value of $`\rho`$).
Furthermore,
([26](#S4.E26 "In IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
suggests that
$`\mathcal{L}(\lambda)=\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\rho=\rho^{*}(\lambda))`$,
and is therefore also bounded by the right-hand side of
([83](#chapterx1.A2.E83 "In 0.B.3 An additional property of LM-ELBO ‣ Appendix 0.B Supplementary Properties and Proofs of LM-ELBO ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
thus proving the right inequality in
([82](#chapterx1.A2.E82 "In 0.B.3 An additional property of LM-ELBO ‣ Appendix 0.B Supplementary Properties and Proofs of LM-ELBO ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")).

#### 0.B.4 Convergence Assurance for Gradient Hybrid CAVI

Using LM-ELBO can establish a convergence assurance for a specific class
of CAVI algorithm. Standard CAVI iteratively optimises $`q(\theta)`$ and
$`q(X)`$ with optimal updates like
([25](#S4.E25 "In IV-A Definition of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")).
Each update ensures a non-negative increment of ELBO and hence
guarantees the convergence. However, if the optimal update for one of
the two variational distributions, e.g., the $`q(X)`$, lacks an
analytical solution, an intuitive workaround is implementing one step of
the gradient ascent update of $`q(X;\lambda)`$ (using
$`\nabla_{\lambda}\mathcal{F}`$) while keeping $`q(\theta)`$ fixed; and
then use the optimal update for $`q^{*}(\theta)`$ in
([25](#S4.E25 "In IV-A Definition of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
for the next update step. The convergence of such a modified algorithm
isn’t immediately apparent. Nonetheless, by applying Property 3 in
Section
[IV-B](#S4.SS2 "IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
we recognise that the algorithm essentially performs successive gradient
updates $`\nabla_{\lambda}\mathcal{L}(\lambda)`$ for $`\lambda`$,
assuring convergence since $`\mathcal{L}(\lambda)`$ is a valid objective
function and gradient ascent ensures the convergence. This convergence
assurance can be extended to other hybride CAVI method using different
optimisation technique (e.g., the stochastic and/or natural gradient as
proved in \[[3](#biba.bib3)\]), provided that a similar property to
Property 3 can be established.

## Appendix 0.C Properties of local LM-ELBO and proofs

Here we list 5 properties of local LM-ELBO
$`\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n})`$ (defined in
([39](#S5.E39 "In V-C Decentralisation of LM-ELBO for Multi-sensor Fusion ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")))
as mentioned in Section
[V-C1](#S5.SS3.SSS1 "V-C1 Properties of local LM-ELBO ℒ_𝑠⁢(𝜆_𝑛) ‣ V-C Decentralisation of LM-ELBO for Multi-sensor Fusion ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
along with the corresponding proofs. Recall that in Section
[V-C1](#S5.SS3.SSS1 "V-C1 Properties of local LM-ELBO ℒ_𝑠⁢(𝜆_𝑛) ‣ V-C Decentralisation of LM-ELBO for Multi-sensor Fusion ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
$`\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n})`$ is denoted as the parameter value that
reproduces $`q_{n}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{s})`$ in
([36](#S5.E36 "In V-C Decentralisation of LM-ELBO for Multi-sensor Fusion ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
with $`\lambda_{n}`$ held fixed, i.e.,
$`q_{n}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{s})=q_{n}(\theta_{n}^{s};\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n}))`$.
These 5 properties highlights the relationship between
$`\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n})`$ in
([39](#S5.E39 "In V-C Decentralisation of LM-ELBO for Multi-sensor Fusion ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"))
and $`\mathcal{F}_{s}(\lambda_{n},\rho_{n}^{s})`$ in
([35](#S5.E35 "In V-B Justification for use of LM-ELBO over Original ELBO ‣ V Decentralised Gradient-based Variational inference Framework for Sensor Fusion ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks")),
and they mirror the corresponding five properties in Section
[IV-B](#S4.SS2 "IV-B Properties of LM-ELBO ‣ IV Locally Maximised ELBO for General Variational Inference Tasks ‣ Decentralised Variational Inference Frameworks for Multi-object Tracking on Sensor Networks"),
with $`\lambda,\rho,\rho^{*}(\lambda)`$ replaced by
$`\lambda_{n},\rho_{n}^{s},\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n})`$, and
$`\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L}`$ replaced by
$`\mathcal{F}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{s}`$.

###### Property 0.C.1.

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n})=`$ | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{s}(\lambda_{n},\rho_{n}^{s}=\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n})),`$ |  | (84) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n})=`$ | $`\displaystyle\max_{\rho_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{F}_{s}(\lambda_{n},\rho_{n}^{s}).`$ |  | (85) |

###### Property 0.C.2.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\rho_{n}^{s}}\mathcal{F}_{s}(\lambda_{n},\rho_{n}^{s})|_{\rho_{n}^{s}=\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n})}=0.`$ |  | (86) |

###### Property 0.C.3.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\lambda_{n}}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n})=\nabla_{\lambda_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{s}(\lambda_{n},\rho_{n}^{s})|_{\rho_{n}^{s}=\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n})}.`$ |  | (87) |

###### Property 0.C.4.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle{\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}}^{2}\mathcal{L}_{s}(\lambda_{n})={\nabla}_{\lambda_{n}}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{s}(\lambda_{n},\rho_{n}^{s})|_{\rho_{n}^{s}=\rho_{n}^{s*}(\lambda_{n})}+P,`$ |  | (88) |

where $`P`$ is a positive semi-definite matrix.

###### Property 0.C.5.
````
